title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" 1882–1969)</li><li>27 June (Church of England in Common Worship)</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated in</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/Latin_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin Christianity">Latin Christianity</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Catholicism">Eastern Catholicism</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy" title="Eastern Orthodoxy">Eastern Orthodoxy</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Orthodoxy">Oriental Orthodoxy</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglicanism</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheranism</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Title as Saint</th><td class="infobox-data">The Pillar of Faith; Seal of all the Fathers; Bishop, Confessor, Bishop of Alexandria, Teacher of the Faith and also (in the Catholic Church) Doctor of the Church</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Attributes</th><td class="infobox-data">Vested as a <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> with <a href="/wiki/Phelonion" title="Phelonion">phelonion</a> and <a href="/wiki/Omophorion" title="Omophorion">omophorion</a>, and usually with his head covered in the manner of Egyptian monastics (sometimes the head covering has a <a href="/wiki/Polystavrion" class="mw-redirect" title="Polystavrion">polystavrion</a> pattern), he usually is depicted holding a <a href="/wiki/Gospel_Book" title="Gospel Book">Gospel Book</a> or a <a href="/wiki/Scroll" title="Scroll">scroll</a>, with his right hand raised in blessing.</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Patronage</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a></td></tr></tbody></table>

</div></td>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below" style="padding-top:0.1em;">
<span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:P_christianity.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/16px-P_christianity.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/24px-P_christianity.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/32px-P_christianity.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="360" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Christianity" title="Portal:Christianity">Christianity&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Oriental_Orthodox_sidebar" title="Template:Oriental Orthodox sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Oriental_Orthodox_sidebar" title="Template talk:Oriental Orthodox sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Oriental_Orthodox_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Cyril of Alexandria</b> (<a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Greek language">Ancient Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Κύριλλος Ἀλεξανδρείας</span>; <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a>: <span lang="cop">Ⲡⲁⲡⲁ Ⲕⲩⲣⲓⲗⲗⲟⲩ ⲁ̅ also ⲡⲓ̀ⲁⲅⲓⲟⲥ Ⲕⲓⲣⲓⲗⲗⲟⲥ</span>; <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr>&#160;376 – 444) was the <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Alexandria" title="Patriarch of Alexandria">Patriarch of Alexandria</a> from 412 to 444.<sup id="cite_ref-Chapman_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chapman-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He was enthroned when the city was at the height of its influence and power within the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a>. Cyril wrote extensively and was a major player in the <a href="/wiki/Christological_controversies" class="mw-redirect" title="Christological controversies">Christological controversies</a> of the late-4th and 5th centuries. He was a central figure in the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus" title="Council of Ephesus">Council of Ephesus</a> in 431, which led to the deposition of <a href="/wiki/Nestorius" title="Nestorius">Nestorius</a> as <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople" title="Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople">Patriarch of Constantinople</a>.
Cyril is counted among the <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a> and also as a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>, and his reputation within the <a href="/wiki/Christian_world" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian world">Christian world</a> has resulted in his titles <i>Pillar of Faith</i> and <i>Seal of all the Fathers</i>. 
The <a href="/wiki/Nestorianism" title="Nestorianism">Nestorian</a> bishops at their synod at the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Ephesus" class="mw-redirect" title="First Council of Ephesus">Council of Ephesus</a> declared him a heretic, labelling him as a "monster, born and educated for the destruction of the church."<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cyril is well known for his dispute with Nestorius and his supporter, Patriarch <a href="/wiki/John_I_of_Antioch" title="John I of Antioch">John of Antioch</a>, whom Cyril excluded from the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus" title="Council of Ephesus">Council of Ephesus</a> for arriving late. He is also known for his expulsion of <a href="/wiki/Novatianism" title="Novatianism">Novatians</a> and Jews from Alexandria and for inflaming tensions that led to the murder of the <a href="/wiki/Hellenistic_philosophy" title="Hellenistic philosophy">Hellenistic philosopher</a> <a href="/wiki/Hypatia" title="Hypatia">Hypatia</a> by a Christian mob. Historians disagree over the extent of his responsibility in this.
</p><p>Cyril tried to oblige the pious Christian emperor Theodosius II (AD 408–450) to himself by dedicating his <a href="/wiki/Date_of_Easter" title="Date of Easter">Paschal table</a> to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMosshammer2008193–194_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMosshammer2008193–194-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Cyril's Paschal table was provided with a <a href="/wiki/Metonic_cycle" title="Metonic cycle">Metonic</a> basic structure in the form of a 19-year lunar cycle adopted by him around AD 425, which was very different from the first Metonic 19-year lunar cycle invented around AD 260 by <a href="/wiki/Anatolius_of_Laodicea" title="Anatolius of Laodicea">Anatolius</a>, but exactly equal to the lunar cycle which had been introduced around AD 412 by <a href="/wiki/Annianus_of_Alexandria" title="Annianus of Alexandria">Annianus</a>; the <a href="/wiki/Julian_calendar" title="Julian calendar">Julian</a> equivalent of this <a href="/wiki/Coptic_calendar" title="Coptic calendar">Alexandrian</a> cycle adopted by Cyril and nowadays referred to as the 'classical (Alexandrian) 19-year lunar cycle' would emerge a century later in Rome as the basic structure of <a href="/wiki/Dionysius_Exiguus" title="Dionysius Exiguus">Dionysius Exiguus</a>’ Paschal table (AD 525).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZuidhoek201967–74_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuidhoek201967–74-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> did not commemorate Saint Cyril in the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Tridentine Calendar">Tridentine Calendar</a>: it added his feast only in 1882, assigning to it the date of 9 February. This date is used by the <a href="/wiki/Western_Rite_Orthodoxy" title="Western Rite Orthodoxy">Western Rite Orthodox Church</a>. Yet the 1969 Catholic Calendar revision moved it to 27 June, considered to be the day of the saint's death, as celebrated by the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Orthodox Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The same date has been chosen for the Lutheran calendar. The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a> and <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine</a> <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Catholic">Catholic</a> Churches celebrate his feast day on 9 June and also, together with <a href="/wiki/Pope_Athanasius_I_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Athanasius I of Alexandria">Pope Athanasius I of Alexandria</a>, on 18 January.
</p><p>Cyril is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a commemoration on 27 June.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Little is known for certain of Cyril's early life. He was born circa 376, in the town of Didouseya, Egypt, modern-day <a href="/wiki/El-Mahalla_El-Kubra" class="mw-redirect" title="El-Mahalla El-Kubra">El-Mahalla El-Kubra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> A few years after his birth, his maternal uncle <a href="/wiki/Theophilus_I_of_Alexandria" title="Theophilus I of Alexandria">Theophilus</a> rose to the powerful position of <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Alexandria" title="Patriarch of Alexandria">Patriarch of Alexandria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dictionary_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dictionary-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> His mother remained close to her brother and under his guidance, Cyril was well educated. His writings show his knowledge of Christian writers of his day, including <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Eusebius of Caesarea">Eusebius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Didymus_the_Blind" title="Didymus the Blind">Didymus the Blind</a>, and writers of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Alexandria" title="Church of Alexandria">Church of Alexandria</a>. He received the formal Christian education standard for his day: he studied grammar from age twelve to fourteen (390–392),<sup id="cite_ref-st-takla.org_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-st-takla.org-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> rhetoric and humanities from fifteen to twenty (393–397) and finally theology and biblical studies (398–402).<sup id="cite_ref-st-takla.org_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-st-takla.org-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 403, he accompanied his uncle to attend the "<a href="/wiki/Synod_of_the_Oak" title="Synod of the Oak">Synod of the Oak</a>" in Constantinople,<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> which deposed <a href="/wiki/John_Chrysostom" title="John Chrysostom">John Chrysostom</a> as Archbishop of Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The prior year, Theophilus had been summoned by the emperor to <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> to apologize before a <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synod</a>, over which Chrysostom would preside, on account of several charges which were brought against him by certain Egyptian monks. Theophilus had them persecuted as <a href="/wiki/Origen_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Origen of Alexandria">Origenists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Placing himself at the head of soldiers and armed servants, Theophilus had marched against the monks, burned their dwellings, and ill-treated those whom he captured.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Theophilus arrived at Constantinople with twenty-nine of his suffragan bishops, and conferring with those opposed to the Archbishop, drafted a long list of largely unfounded accusations against Chrysostom,<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> who refused to recognize the legality of a synod in which his open enemies were judges. Chrysostom was subsequently deposed.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patriarch_of_Alexandria">Patriarch of Alexandria</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Patriarch of Alexandria">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Theophilus died on 15 October 412, and Cyril was made <a href="/wiki/Pope_(word)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope (word)">Pope</a> or <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Alexandria" title="Patriarch of Alexandria">Patriarch of Alexandria</a> on 18 October 412, but only after a riot between his supporters and those of his rival Archdeacon Timotheus. According to <a href="/wiki/Socrates_of_Constantinople" title="Socrates of Constantinople">Socrates Scholasticus</a>, the Alexandrians were always rioting.<sup id="cite_ref-Chapman_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chapman-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thus, Cyril followed his uncle in a position that had become powerful and influential, rivalling that of the prefect in a time of turmoil and frequently violent conflict between the cosmopolitan city's <a href="/wiki/Paganism" title="Paganism">pagan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jewish</a>, and Christian inhabitants.<sup id="cite_ref-library_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-library-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He began to exert his authority by causing the churches of the <a href="/wiki/Novatianism" title="Novatianism">Novatianists</a> to be closed and their sacred vessels to be seized.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Controversies">Controversies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Controversies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dispute_with_the_Prefect">Dispute with the Prefect</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Dispute with the Prefect">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Orestes_(prefect)" class="mw-redirect" title="Orestes (prefect)">Orestes</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Praefectus_augustalis" class="mw-redirect" title="Praefectus augustalis">Praefectus augustalis</a></i> of the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Diocese_of_Egypt" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Diocese of Egypt">Diocese of Egypt</a>, steadfastly resisted Cyril's ecclesiastical encroachment upon secular prerogatives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel200434_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel200434-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tension between the parties increased when in 415, Orestes published an edict that outlined new regulations regarding mime shows and dancing exhibitions in the city, which attracted large crowds and were commonly prone to civil disorder of varying degrees. Crowds gathered to read the edict shortly after it was posted in the city's theater. Cyril sent the <i>grammaticus</i> Hierax to discover the content of the edict. The edict angered Christians as well as Jews. At one such gathering, Hierax read the edict and applauded the new regulations, prompting a disturbance. Many people felt that Hierax was attempting to incite the crowd—particularly the Jews—into sedition.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Orestes had Hierax tortured in public in a theatre. This order had two aims: one to quell the riot, the other to mark Orestes' authority over Cyril.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel200434_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel200434-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Socrates_of_Constantinople" title="Socrates of Constantinople">Socrates Scholasticus</a> recounts that upon hearing of Hierex's severe and public punishment, Cyril threatened to retaliate against the Jews of Alexandria with "the utmost severities" if the harassment of Christians did not cease immediately. In response to Cyril's threat, the Jews of Alexandria grew even more furious, eventually resorting to violence against the Christians. They plotted to flush the Christians out at night by running through the streets claiming that the Church of Alexander was on fire. When Christians responded to what they were led to believe was the burning down of their church, "the Jews immediately fell upon and slew them" by using rings to recognize one another in the dark and killing everyone else in sight. When the morning came, Cyril, along with many of his followers, took to the city's synagogues in search of the perpetrators of the massacre.<sup id="cite_ref-Scholasticus_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scholasticus-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Socrates Scholasticus, after Cyril rounded up all the Jews in Alexandria he ordered them to be stripped of all possessions, banished them from Alexandria, and allowed their goods to be pillaged by the remaining citizens of Alexandria. Scholasticus alleges that all the Jews of Alexandria were banished, while <a href="/wiki/John_of_Niki%C3%BB" title="John of Nikiû">John of Nikiû</a> says it was only those involved in the ambush and massacre. Susan Wessel says that, while it is not clear whether Scholasticus was a Novationist (whose churches Cyril had closed), he was apparently sympathetic towards them, and repeatedly accuses clear Cyril of abusing his episcopal power by infringing on the rights and duties of the secular authorities. Wessel says, however, "...Socrates probably does not provide accurate and unambiguous information about Cyril's relationship to imperial authority".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel200422_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel200422-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nonetheless, with Cyril's banishment of the Jews, however many, "Orestes [...] was filled with great indignation at these transactions, and was excessively grieved that a city of such magnitude should have been suddenly bereft of so large a portion of its population."<sup id="cite_ref-Scholasticus_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scholasticus-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Because of this, the feud between Cyril and Orestes intensified, and both men wrote to the emperor regarding the situation. Eventually, Cyril attempted to reach out to Orestes through several peace overtures, including attempted mediation and, when that failed, showed him the Gospels, which he interpreted to indicate that the religious authority of Cyril would require Orestes' acquiescence in the bishop's policy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel200435_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel200435-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, Orestes remained unmoved by such gestures.
</p><p>This refusal almost cost Orestes his life. <a href="/wiki/Wadi_El_Natrun" title="Wadi El Natrun">Nitrian monks</a> came from the desert and instigated a riot against Orestes among the population of Alexandria. These monks had resorted to violence 15 years before, during a controversy between Theophilus (Cyril's uncle) and the "<a href="/wiki/Tall_Brothers" title="Tall Brothers">Tall Brothers</a>"; the monks assaulted Orestes and accused him of being a pagan. Orestes rejected the accusations, showing that he had been baptised by the Archbishop of Constantinople. A monk named <a href="/wiki/Ammonius_(Alexandrian_monk)" title="Ammonius (Alexandrian monk)">Ammonius</a> threw a stone hitting Orestes in the head. The prefect had Ammonius tortured to death, whereupon the Patriarch allegedly honored him as a martyr. However, at least according to Scholasticus, the Christian community displayed a general lack of enthusiasm for Ammonius's case for martyrdom. The prefect then wrote to the emperor <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_II" title="Theodosius II">Theodosius II</a>, as did Cyril.<sup id="cite_ref-ss7_14_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ss7_14-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel200435–36_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel200435–36-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Murder_of_Hypatia">Murder of Hypatia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Murder of Hypatia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Hypatia" title="Hypatia">Hypatia</a></div>
<p>The Prefect <a href="/wiki/Orestes_(prefect)" class="mw-redirect" title="Orestes (prefect)">Orestes</a> enjoyed the political backing of <a href="/wiki/Hypatia" title="Hypatia">Hypatia</a>, an astronomer, philosopher and mathematician who had considerable <a href="/wiki/Moral_authority" title="Moral authority">moral authority</a> in the city of Alexandria, and who had extensive influence. Indeed, many students from wealthy and influential families came to Alexandria purposely to study privately with <a href="/wiki/Hypatia" title="Hypatia">Hypatia</a> , and many of these later attained high posts in government and the Church. Several Christians thought that Hypatia's influence had caused Orestes to reject all conciliatory offerings by Cyril. Modern historians think that Orestes had cultivated his relationship with Hypatia to strengthen a bond with the pagan community of Alexandria, as he had done with the Jewish one, in order to better manage the tumultuous political life of the Egyptian capital.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> A mob, led by a <a href="/wiki/Lector" title="Lector">lector</a> named Peter, took Hypatia from her chariot and murdered her, hacking her body apart and burning the pieces outside the city walls.<sup id="cite_ref-ss7_15_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ss7_15-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Neoplatonist historian <a href="/wiki/Damascius" title="Damascius">Damascius</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr>&#160;458 – <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr>&#160;538) was "anxious to exploit the scandal of Hypatia's death", and attributed responsibility for her murder to Bishop Cyril and his Christian followers.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Damascius's account of the Christian murder of Hypatia is the sole historical source attributing direct responsibility to Bishop Cyril.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDzielska199518_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDzielska199518-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Some modern studies, as well as the 2009 <a href="/wiki/Hypatia" title="Hypatia">Hypatia</a> <a href="/wiki/Biopic" class="mw-redirect" title="Biopic">biopic</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Agora_(film)" title="Agora (film)">Agora</a></i> represent Hypatia as falling casualty to a conflict between two Christian factions, one peaceful and moderate and led by Orestes, with the support of Hypatia, and <a href="/wiki/Fundamentalist" class="mw-redirect" title="Fundamentalist">fundamentalist</a> faction enforced by <a href="/wiki/Death_squads" class="mw-redirect" title="Death squads">death squads</a> and led by Patriarch Cyril.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDzielska1995xi,_157_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDzielska1995xi,_157-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> According to lexicographer <a href="/wiki/William_Smith_(lexicographer)" title="William Smith (lexicographer)">William Smith</a>, "She was accused of too much familiarity with Orestes, prefect of Alexandria, and the charge spread among the clergy, who took up the notion that she interrupted the friendship of Orestes with their archbishop, Cyril."<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Scholasticus, alleges that Hypatia fell "victim to the political jealousy which at the time prevailed" and that news of Hypatia's murder, "brought no small disgrace", not only to Patriarch Cyril but to the whole Christian Church in Alexandria, "for murder and slaughter and all such things are altogether opposed to the Christian religion."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFortescue2007165_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFortescue2007165-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Byzantinist" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantinist">Byzantinist</a> Fr. <a href="/wiki/Adrian_Fortescue" title="Adrian Fortescue">Adrian Fortescue</a>, however, "In March of 415, a mob of Christians, led by the <a href="/wiki/Parabolani" class="mw-redirect" title="Parabolani">Parabolani</a> and by a Lector named Peter, cruelly tore her to pieces on the steps of a church. Various writers have suggested more or less plainly that the Patriarch was involved in the crime. Sokrates does not say so plainly, but he implies it and adds a solemn moral reflection. As a matter of fact, not only is there no sort of evidence that he had anything to do with it, there are positive reasons for knowing that he had not. After the murder, a deputation of citizens went to Constantinople to petition the Emperor to prevent such horrors in the future and to put down the disorderly Parabolani, and the first means they urge for that purpose is that the Patriarch should stay in the city (Orestes wanted him banished). Moreover, if ever a man had bitter enemies it was Cyril. Wilful murder was considered just as unsuitable conduct for bishops in the fifth century as it is now. Why, during all the fierce conflict with the <a href="/wiki/Nestorians" class="mw-redirect" title="Nestorians">Nestorians</a>, when they brought up every possible charge against him, did no one think of calling him Hypatia's murderer? Although to accuse our Saint of this horrid story is a gross <a href="/wiki/Calumny" class="mw-redirect" title="Calumny">calumny</a>, there is no doubt that in other ways he did give annoyance to the government."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFortescue2007165–166_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFortescue2007165–166-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflict_with_Nestorius">Conflict with Nestorius</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Conflict with Nestorius">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Another major conflict was between the <a href="/wiki/Alexandrian_school" title="Alexandrian school">Alexandrian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Antiochian_school" class="mw-redirect" title="Antiochian school">Antiochian</a> schools of ecclesiastical reflection, piety, and discourse. This long running conflict widened with the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Constantinople#Dispute_concerning_the_third_canon" title="First Council of Constantinople">third canon of the First Council of Constantinople</a> which granted the see of Constantinople primacy over the older sees of Alexandria and Antioch. Thus, the struggle between the sees of Alexandria and Antioch now included Constantinople. The conflict came to a head in 428 after <a href="/wiki/Nestorius" title="Nestorius">Nestorius</a>, who originated in Antioch, was made Archbishop of Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cyril gained an opportunity to restore Alexandria's pre-eminence over both Antioch and Constantinople when an Antiochine priest who was in Constantinople at Nestorius' behest began to preach against calling <a href="/wiki/Mary_(mother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary (mother of Jesus)">Mary</a> the "Mother of God" (<i><a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Theotokos</a></i>). As the term "Mother of God" had long been attached to Mary, the laity in Constantinople complained against the priest. Rather than repudiating the priest, Nestorius intervened on his behalf. Nestorius argued that Mary was neither a "Mother of Man" nor "<a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Mother of God</a>" as these referred to <a href="/wiki/Christology#Person_of_Christ" title="Christology">Christ's two natures</a>; rather, Mary was the "Mother of Christ" (Greek: <i><a href="/wiki/Christotokos" title="Christotokos">Christotokos</a></i>). Christ, according to Nestorius, was the conjunction of the Godhead with his "temple" (which Nestorius was fond of calling his human nature). The controversy seemed to be centered on the issue of the suffering of Christ. Cyril maintained that the Son of God or the divine Word, truly suffered "in the flesh."<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> However, Nestorius claimed that the Son of God was altogether incapable of suffering, even within his union with the flesh.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Eusebius of Dorylaeum went so far as to accuse Nestorius of <a href="/wiki/Adoptionism" title="Adoptionism">adoptionism</a>. By this time, news of the controversy in the capital had reached Alexandria. At Easter 429 A.D., Cyril wrote a letter to the Egyptian monks warning them of Nestorius's views. A copy of this letter reached Constantinople where Nestorius preached a sermon against it. This began a series of letters between Cyril and Nestorius which gradually became more strident in tone. Finally, Emperor <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_II" title="Theodosius II">Theodosius II</a> convoked the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Ephesus" class="mw-redirect" title="First Council of Ephesus">Council of Ephesus</a> (in 431) to solve the dispute. Cyril selected Ephesus<sup id="cite_ref-st-takla.org_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-st-takla.org-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> as the venue since it supported the veneration of Mary. The council was convoked before Nestorius's supporters from Antioch and Syria had arrived and thus Nestorius refused to attend when summoned. Predictably, the Council ordered the deposition and exile of Nestorius for heresy.
</p><p>However, when <a href="/wiki/John_I_of_Antioch" title="John I of Antioch">John of Antioch</a> and the other pro-Nestorius bishops finally reached Ephesus, they assembled their own Council, condemned Cyril for heresy, deposed him from his see, and labelled him as a "monster, born and educated for the destruction of the church".<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Theodosius, by now old enough to hold power by himself, annulled the verdict of the Council and arrested Cyril, but Cyril eventually escaped. Having fled to Egypt, Cyril bribed Theodosius's courtiers, and sent a mob led by <a href="/wiki/Dalmatius_of_Constantinople" title="Dalmatius of Constantinople">Dalmatius</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Hermit" title="Hermit">hermit</a>, to besiege Theodosius's palace, and shout abuse; the emperor eventually gave in, sending Nestorius into minor exile (Upper Egypt).<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
Cyril died about 444, but the controversies were to continue for decades, from the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_Ephesus" title="Second Council of Ephesus">"Robber Synod" of Ephesus</a> (449) to the <a href="/wiki/Fourth_Ecumenical_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Fourth Ecumenical Council">Council of Chalcedon</a> (451) and beyond.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Icon_St._Cyril_of_Alexandria.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Icon_St._Cyril_of_Alexandria.jpg/220px-Icon_St._Cyril_of_Alexandria.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Icon_St._Cyril_of_Alexandria.jpg/330px-Icon_St._Cyril_of_Alexandria.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Icon_St._Cyril_of_Alexandria.jpg/440px-Icon_St._Cyril_of_Alexandria.jpg 2x" data-file-width="827" data-file-height="1101" /></a><figcaption>Icon of St. Cyril of Alexandria</figcaption></figure>
<p>Cyril regarded the embodiment of <a href="/wiki/God" title="God">God</a> in the person of <a href="/wiki/Jesus_Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesus Christ">Jesus Christ</a> to be so mystically powerful that it spread out from the body of the <a href="/wiki/God-man_(Christianity)" title="God-man (Christianity)">God-man</a> into the rest of the race, to reconstitute human nature into a graced and deified condition of the saints, one that promised immortality and <a href="/wiki/Transfiguration_of_Jesus" title="Transfiguration of Jesus">transfiguration</a> to believers. <a href="/wiki/Nestorius" title="Nestorius">Nestorius</a>, on the other hand, saw the incarnation as primarily a moral and ethical example to the faithful, to follow in the footsteps of Jesus. Cyril's constant stress was on the simple idea that it was God who walked the streets of Nazareth (hence Mary was <i><a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Theotokos</a></i>, meaning "God bearer", which became in Latin "Mater Dei or Dei Genitrix", or Mother of God), and God who had appeared in a transfigured humanity. Nestorius spoke of the distinct "Jesus the man" and "the divine <a href="/wiki/Logos" title="Logos">Logos</a>" in ways that Cyril thought were too <a href="/wiki/Dichotomy" title="Dichotomy">dichotomous</a>, widening the ontological gap between man and God in a way that some of his contemporaries believed would annihilate the person of Christ.
</p><p>The main issue that prompted this dispute between Cyril and Nestorius was the question which arose at the Council of Constantinople: What exactly was the being to which Mary gave birth? Cyril affirmed that the Holy Trinity consists of a singular divine nature, essence, and being (<i>ousia</i>) in three distinct aspects, instantiations, or subsistencies of being (<i>hypostases</i>). These distinct hypostases are the Father or God in Himself, the Son or Word (<i>Logos</i>), and the Holy Spirit. Then, when the Son became flesh and entered the world, the pre-Incarnate divine nature and assumed human nature both remained, but became <i>united</i> in the person of Jesus. This resulted in the <a href="/wiki/Miaphysitism" title="Miaphysitism">miaphysite</a> slogan "One Nature united out of two" being used to encapsulate the theological position of this Alexandrian bishop. 
</p><p>According to Cyril's theology, there were two states for the Son of God: the state that existed <i>prior</i> to the Son (or Word/Logos) becoming enfleshed in the person of Jesus and the state that actually became enfleshed. The Logos Incarnate suffered and died on the Cross, and therefore the Son was able to suffer without suffering. Cyril passionately argued for the continuity of a single subject, God the Word, from the pre-Incarnate state to the Incarnate state. The divine Logos was really present in the flesh and in the world—not merely bestowed upon, semantically affixed to, or morally associated with the man Jesus, as the <a href="/wiki/Adoptionism" title="Adoptionism">adoptionists</a> and, he believed, Nestorius had taught.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mariology">Mariology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Mariology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Cyril of Alexandria became noted in <a href="/wiki/Church_history" title="Church history">Church history</a> because of his spirited fight for the title "<a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Theotokos</a><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>" during the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Ephesus" class="mw-redirect" title="First Council of Ephesus">First Council of Ephesus</a> (431), establishing the ecclesiastically settled basis for all subsequent <a href="/wiki/Mariology" title="Mariology">mariological</a> developments<sup id="cite_ref-HarperOne_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HarperOne-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Prior to the controversy over the theology of <a href="/wiki/Nestorius" title="Nestorius">Nestorius</a>, Cyril rarely if ever used the Mariological title, but theo-political circumstances compelled him as Archbishop of one of the Empire's chief sees, to become involved and develop his theology."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll2000111–114_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll2000111–114-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Beginning in 429 Cyril wrote a series of letters to various ecclesiastical authorities including Nestorius himself, in which he espoused the orthodoxy of "Theotokos". The propriety of the term was justified through appeals to earlier theologians who had used it, like <a href="/wiki/Athanasius_of_Alexandria" title="Athanasius of Alexandria">Athanasius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Basil_of_Caesarea" title="Basil of Caesarea">Basil</a> and <a href="/wiki/Atticus_of_Constantinople" title="Atticus of Constantinople">Atticus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConstas200335_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstas200335-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Following an epistolary exchange with the increasingly unpopular archbishop of Constantinople, in 430 Cyril wrote his famous 12 Anathemas in which anyone who refused to call Mary Theotokos was condemned. The following year over 100 bishops met in council at Ephesus to rule on the disputes. Inbetween sessions at the Council Cyril delivered a number of sermons; some of those attributed to his hand are of disputed authorship, but 6 are recognised as genuine. Homily IV delivered upon the late arrival of western delegates is a particularly striking example of Cyril's developed Mariology.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel2004191_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel2004191-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>It is the foremost expression of Cyril's devotion to Mary, and is one of the first historical attestations of the salutation Χαῖρε ("Hail") being used to invoke the Virgin, a practice later standardised in Byzantine homiletics and hymnography such as the sermons of <a href="/wiki/Chrysippus_of_Jerusalem" title="Chrysippus of Jerusalem">Chrysippus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Basil_of_Seleucia" title="Basil of Seleucia">Basil of Selecucia</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Akathist" title="Akathist">Akathist hymn</a>. Mary, who is credited with calling the council fathers together, embodies for Cyril the paradoxes of orthodox Christology, "container of the uncontained" and "the place for the infinite", among other lauded descriptions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel2004224–225_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel2004224–225-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Cyril's notions of the identity of Christ, therefore, have direct bearing on the identity of Mary. Wessell explains how "Cyril's spatial metaphors construed Mary as a sacred place" and how he "applied metaphors depicting royalty and exaltation to Mary: she was the treasure of the world, the crown of virginity, and the sceptre of orthodoxy."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel2004230_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel2004230-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Subsequently, such praise would become normative in Marian theology.
</p><p>In several of his works, Cyril focuses on the love of Jesus to his mother. On the <a href="/wiki/Cross" title="Cross">Cross</a>, he overcomes his pain and thinks of his mother. At the wedding in <a href="/wiki/Cana" title="Cana">Cana</a>, he bows to her wishes. The conflict with Nestorius was mainly over this issue, and some have argued that it has often been misunderstood. "[T]he debate was not so much about Mary as about Jesus. The question was not what honors were due to Mary, but how one was to speak of the birth of Jesus."<sup id="cite_ref-HarperOne_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HarperOne-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Wessell notes that in Homily V delivered at the Council, Cyril argued that Nestorius' refusal to acknowledge God's incarnate birth from Mary was a blasphemy against Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel2004217–218_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel2004217–218-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> At the same time the close relationship between Christological and Mariological formulations going back to the <a href="/wiki/Cappadocian_Fathers" title="Cappadocian Fathers">Cappadocian Fathers</a> created a climate wherein intellectual argumentation over disputed theology overlapped with blossoming piety. When the Council of Ephesus convened under Cyril's presidency it did so in the newly constructed <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Mary" title="Church of Mary">Church of Mary</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll2000111_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Carroll2000111-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> a venue that contributed to the devotional matrix of the debates. Whereas in the past scholars have often argued that Marian piety and theology only developed in the wake of the conciliar decrees, Shoemaker considers this to be refuted by the picture emerging from liturgical and archaeological evidence.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The substance of Cyril's arumentation was Christological in orientation. His mature Mariology was chiefly in service to this, and to the end of discrediting Nestorius.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel2004224_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel2004224-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Yet Wessel, quoting Homily IV, notes that the enthusiastic praises go beyond the strictly Christological. "She was not only valuable as a vessel storing something sacred but was herself precious and venerated: ‘Is it even possible for people to speak of the celebrated Mary? The virginal womb; O thing of wonder! The marvel strikes me with awe!’" Such sentiments served to further distinguish what Cyril believed to be orthodox theology from that which Nestorius taught, characterising the latter as subversive to both church and empire. As "scepter of orthodoxy", Mary became the standard of Christological fidelity in Cyril's theology; Nestorius's denial of "Theotokos" became the identifiable sign of his impugning of the divinity of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWessel2004230–232_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessel2004230–232-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St. Cyril received an important recognition of his preachings by the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_Constantinople" title="Second Council of Constantinople">Second Council of Constantinople</a> (553 d.C.) which declared;
</p>
<dl><dd>"St. Cyril who announced the right faith of Christians" (Anathematism XIV, Denzinger et Schoenmetzer 437).</dd></dl>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Cyril was a scholarly archbishop and a prolific writer. In the early years of his active life in the Church he wrote several exegetical documents. Among these were: <i>Commentaries on the Old Testament</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> <i>Thesaurus</i>, <i>Discourse Against Arians</i>, <i>Commentary on St. John's Gospel</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> and <i>Dialogues on the Trinity</i>. In 429 as the Christological controversies increased, the output of his writings was so extensive that his opponents could not match it. His writings and his theology have remained central to the tradition of the Fathers and to all Orthodox to this day.
</p>
<ul><li><i><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.ellopos.net/elpenor/greek-texts/fathers/cyril-temples.asp">Becoming Temples of God</a></i> (Ναοὶ θεοῦ χρηματιοῦμεν) (in Greek original and English)</li>
